L43: ## 1 Executive Summary
L44: ## 1.1 Product Introduction
L45: Tisotumab vedotin-tftv is a tissue factor (TF)-directed antibody drug conjugate (ADC). It contains a human anti-TF IgG1 kappa antibody, a protease-cleavable valine-citrulline linker, and a monomethyl auristatin E (MMAE) payload which is a microtubule-disrupting agent. Tisotumab vedotin is not approved for any indication worldwide.
L46: The Applicant proposed the following indication for BLA 761208:
L47: (b) (4)
L48: *This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and durability of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in confirmatory trials.*
L49: The recommended indication for accelerated approval is:
L50: *Tivdak is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer with disease progression on or after chemotherapy.*
L51: *This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and durability of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in confirmatory trials.*
L52: The recommended dose of tisotumab vedotin is 2 mg/kg (up to a maximum of 200 mg for patients \ge100 kg) given IV over 30 minutes every 3 weeks until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
L53: ## 1.2 Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L54: (b) (4)
L55: The review team recommends accelerated approval of tisotumab vedotin
L56: in accordance with 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) Part 601.41, Subpart E for Biological Products Licensing Regulations. This recommendation is primarily based on data from GCT1015-04 (innovaTV 204, NCT03438396), a single arm, international trial in 101 patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer who had received \le2 prior lines of systemic treatment in the metastatic setting, including doublet chemotherapy, with bevacizumab if eligible. Sixty-nine percent of patients had received bevacizumab as part of prior systemic therapy.
L57: The major efficacy outcomes measures were confirmed overall response rate (ORR) as assessed by an independent review committee (IRC) according to RECIST v1.1, and DOR. Confirmed ORR was 23.8% (95% CI: 15.9%, 33.3%). Median DOR was 8.3 months (95% CI: 4.2, NR). These results
L58: represent an advantage compared to available therapies and are reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit in the indicated patient population.
L59: Available therapies in this treatment setting include single agent chemotherapies with ORRs ranging from 5-21% and median DORs ranging from 2-4 months. One important caveat in interpreting these ORRs/DORs is that these data are from small trials conducted prior to establishment of a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab as standard-of-care first-line treatment for patients in the U.S. The efficacy of these chemotherapy agents (as measured by ORR and DOR) in the current era of second-line or later treatment for cervical cancer following first-line treatment with a chemotherapy doublet and bevacizumab is unknown. However, the review team expects that the efficacy of several of the available single agent chemotherapies would be less in the current treatment landscape, after bevacizumab-containing doublet chemotherapy.
L60: As a result, the review team concludes that tisotumab vedotin represents a new treatment option for patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer who have received \le 2 prior lines of systemic treatment in the metastatic setting, including doublet chemotherapy, with bevacizumab if eligible. This patient population has a significant unmet medical need for better therapies.
L61: FDA review team recommends granting accelerated approval to tisotumab vedotin for the following indication:
L62: *Tivdax is a tissue factor-directed antibody and microtubule inhibitor conjugate indicated for the treatment of adult patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer with disease progression on or after chemotherapy.*
L63: *This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and durability of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in confirmatory trials.*
L64: ## 1.3 Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L65: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L66: Cervical cancer is the fourth most common cancer in women worldwide. In the U.S., there will be an estimated 14,480 new cases and 4,290 deaths from cervical cancer in 2021. Almost all cases of cervical cancer are associated with HPV infection. The incidence of cervical cancer in the U.S. has fallen over the past few decades due to the implementation of screening with Pap tests. However, once diagnosed, cervical cancer is a serious and life-threatening condition with an estimated 5-year survival of 66%. If detected at an early stage, cervical cancer is potentially curable. Recurrent cervical cancer that is not amenable to local intervention (surgery and/or radiation) or metastatic cervical cancer is incurable, and treatment is palliative.
L67: For early-stage disease, patients will typically receive surgery or radiation treatment with chemotherapy. For recurrent or metastatic disease, patients in the US will typically receive first-line systemic treatment with a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab. For second and later-line therapy, treatment options are limited and include single agent chemotherapies such as docetaxel, gemcitabine, ifosfamide, irinotecan, and others, with modest ORRs of 5%-21% and limited median DORs of 2-4 months. An additional challenge with these treatment options is that their efficacy following first-line treatment with a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab is unknown. Patients with tumors that express PD-L1 (CPS\ge1) may receive pembrolizumab which is under an accelerated approval and thus not considered available therapy, based on an ORR of 14% and a median DOR not reached.
L68: The efficacy and safety assessment for tisotumab vedotin was primarily based on results from GCT1015-04, a single arm, international trial which enrolled 101 patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer. Patients had received \le2 prior lines of treatment in the recurrent or metastatic setting, including a chemotherapy doublet, with bevacizumab, if eligible to receive bevacizumab. Patients received tisotumab vedotin 2 mg/kg intravenously every 3 weeks until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Tumor assessments occurred every 6 weeks for the first 30 weeks, and every 12 weeks thereafter. The primary endpoint was confirmed ORR assessed by IRC according to RECIST v1.1. DOR was a secondary endpoint.
L69: The age distribution of patients on GCT1015-04 was generally representative of a U.S.-based cervical cancer population, with a median age of 50 years (range: 31 years, 78 years), and 13% of patients \ge 65 years. In terms of disease and treatment characteristics, the patient population was relevant to a U.S. based population with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer. Enrolled patients included 68% with squamous cell carcinoma and 27% with adenocarcinoma. Seventy percent of patients had received 1 prior line of treatment in the metastatic setting and 30%
L70: had received 2 prior lines of treatment in the metastatic setting. Most patients (69%) had received prior bevacizumab, including 63% who received it in combination with doublet chemotherapy for first-line recurrent or metastatic disease.
L71: Confirmed ORR as per IRC according to RECIST v1.1 was 23.8% (95% CI: 15.9%, 33.3%) in 101 patients treated with tisotumab. Median DOR was 8.3 months (95% CI: 4.2, NR). ORR results were generally consistent across subgroups, except for in patients \ge 65 years old. No patient \ge65 years experienced a tumor response, however FDA notes that only 13 patients \ge65 years were enrolled.
L72: The safety profile of tisotumab vedotin generally reflects the TF target as well as the MMAE payload. Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 4% of patients and included septic shock and pneumonitis. Serious adverse reactions occurred in 43% of patients and included ileus (6%), hemorrhage (5%), pneumonia (4%), sepsis (3%), constipation (3%), pyrexia (3%), and peripheral neuropathy (3%). The most common adverse reactions (\ge30%) in GCT1015-04 included fatigue, nausea, peripheral neuropathy, alopecia, epistaxis, conjunctival adverse reactions, and hemorrhage. Adverse reactions leading to dose discontinuations occurred in 13% of patients and adverse reactions leading to dose reductions occurred in 23% of patients.
L73: Ocular toxicity is an important safety signal associated with tisotumab vedotin and affected 55% of patients enrolled to GCT1015-04, including 6% of patients with ulcerative keratitis, including one patient with a corneal ulcer that perforated and required corneal transplantation. There is a Boxed Warning for ocular toxicity in the tisotumab vedotin USPI to alert providers to the risk of patients developing severe ocular toxicity including corneal ulcers. Patients and providers should adhere to the premedication and required eye care plan outlined in the tisotumab vedotin USPI, and patients should be promptly referred to an eye care professional for any new or worsening ocular symptoms.
L74: Other important safety signals associated with tisotumab vedotin include peripheral neuropathy, hemorrhage, pneumonitis, and embryo-fetal toxicity, which are listed under Warnings and Precautions in the tisotumab vedotin USPI.
L75: Patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer have limited therapeutic options following first-line treatment with a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab. Available therapies in this treatment setting include single agent chemotherapies with ORRs ranging from 5-21% and median DORs ranging from 2-4 months. These data are from small trials conducted prior to establishment of a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab as standard-of-care first-line treatment for patients in the U.S. Given that all patients who received tisotumab vedotin on GCT1015-04 received first-line doublet chemotherapy and 69% of patients had prior bevacizumab as per U.S. standard of care, the review team considers that the efficacy of tisotumab vedotin demonstrates an improvement compared to available therapies, and these results are reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit. Tisotumab vedotin demonstrated acceptable safety and tolerability in a patient population with a
L76: serious and life-threatening illness and limited therapeutic options. Adverse reactions are manageable through labeling which includes a boxed warning for ocular toxicity, required premedication, dose modifications, and supportive therapy.
L77: Overall, the benefit-risk assessment for tisotumab vedotin is favorable. There is a post-marketing requirement to submit results from ongoing trial SGNTV-003/innovaTV 301, a randomized trial comparing tisotumab vedotin to investigator's choice chemotherapy in patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer, to provide confirmation of clinical benefit.
L78: Tisotumab vedotin is recommended for accelerated approval for the following indication:
L79: *Tivdax is a tissue factor-directed antibody and microtubule inhibitor conjugate indicated for the treatment of adult patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer with disease progression on or after chemotherapy.*
L80: *This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and durability of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in confirmatory trials.*
L81: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Cervical cancer, which is almost always caused by persistent HPV infection, is the fourth most common cancer in women worldwide.
In U.S. women., there are 14,480 new cases and 4,290 cervical cancer-related deaths estimated for 2021.
Metastatic cervical cancer or recurrent cervical cancer which is not amenable to local therapy is not curable.
Recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer is incurable and is a serious and life-threatening condition.
Current Treatment Options	Recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer is not curable, and treatment is palliative.
For first-line treatment in the recurrent or metastatic setting, guideline-based treatment for women in the U.S. is doublet chemotherapy in combination with bevacizumab, if eligible.
There is an unmet medical need for therapies that improve outcomes for patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer following first-line treatment.
L82: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761208}
## {tisotumab vedotin}
L83: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Following first-line treatment, options for subsequent treatment include single agent chemotherapies with relatively low ORRs. Additionally, the efficacy of these therapies after patients receive current standard-of-care first-line treatment with a chemotherapy doublet and bevacizumab is unknown.
For patients with tumors which express PD-L1 (CPS \ge1), pembrolizumab is a treatment option which is under an accelerated approval.

Benefit	GCT1015-04 enrolled 101 patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer who had received \le2 prior systemic treatment regimens in the recurrent or metastatic setting including a chemotherapy doublet, with bevacizumab if eligible.
Confirmed ORR as assessed by IRC according to RECIST v1.1 was 23.8% (95% CI: 15.9, 33.3).
Median DOR was 8.3 months (95% CI: 4.2, NR).
Treatment with tisotumab vedotin in GCT1015-04 resulted in a confirmed ORR and DOR which represent an improvement compared to available therapies for patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer following first-line treatment. ORR and DOR results are reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit.

Risk and Risk Management	In GCT1015-04, the most common (\ge30%) adverse reactions that occurred in patients treated with tisotumab vedotin were hemorrhage, fatigue, nausea, peripheral neuropathy, alopecia, and conjunctival adverse reactions. The most common Grade \ge3 adverse reactions (\ge5%) were: anemia (9%), peripheral neuropathy (7%), fatigue (7%), and hemorrhage (6%).
Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 4% of patients and included septic shock, pneumonitis, sudden death, and multisystem organ failure, each in 1 patient.
The benefit-risk profile of tisotumab vedotin is acceptable for the indicated patient population with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer and limited therapeutic options.
Safe use of tisotumab vedotin can be managed through labeling, which includes ocular toxicity as a boxed warning and the
L84: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Ocular toxicity is an important safety signal associated with tisotumab vedotin, experienced by 55% of patients enrolled to GCT1015-04. In GCT1015-04, 4% of patients developed corneal ulcers (Grade 3 keratitis) and 1% of patients developed a corneal ulcer with perforation (Grade 4 keratitis) requiring corneal transplantation.
There is a boxed warning to alert providers to the potential for severe ocular toxicity in patients receiving tisotumab vedotin in the USPI.
Additional important safety signals associated with tisotumab vedotin include peripheral neuropathy, hemorrhage, pneumonitis, and embryo-fetal toxicity which are listed under Warnings and Precautions in the USPI.
inclusion of peripheral neuropathy, hemorrhage, pneumonitis, and embryo-fetal toxicity under Warnings and Precautions.

L92: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L93: ## 2.1 Analysis of Condition
L94: ## The Applicant's Position:
L95: According to National Cancer Institute (NCI) estimates, more than 291,000 patients are living with invasive cervical cancer in the US, and more than 4,290 women in the United States (US) were estimated to have died from the disease last year (National Cancer Institute (NCI) 2020). An estimated 16% of patients with cervical cancer are first diagnosed with metastatic disease (National Cancer Institute (NCI) 2020). For patients diagnosed in stages I through III, the chances of developing recurrent or metastatic disease after first-line treatment increase with disease stage. Almost half of patients with stage I cervical cancer will see their disease progress to become recurrent or metastatic, while for patients with stage III cervical cancer this figure is nearly 65% (Kantar Health 2020). While uncommon, due to immunization and screening, cervical cancer remains the fourth most common and fourth deadliest cancer in women globally with a 5 year survival rate in the US of 17% (Bray 2018)(National Cancer Institute (NCI) 2020). No curative therapy is available for these patients.
L96: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L97: FDA clarifies that no curative therapy is available for patients with metastatic cervical cancer or for patients with recurrent cervical cancer not amenable to local treatment. Other patients may have curative treatment options. Otherwise, FDA agrees with the Applicant's description of cervical cancer.
L98: ## 2.2 Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L99: ## **First-Line Treatment**
L100: For the vast majority of patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer who are not surgical candidates, palliative chemotherapy represents the only treatment option.
L101: In the first-line, treatment of recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer is paclitaxel + cisplatin or paclitaxel + topotecan combined with bevacizumab (if eligible). Current data indicate that approximately half of all US patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer receive bevacizumab as part of a first-line treatment regimen (Kantar Health).
L102: ## **Treatment in the Second Line and Beyond**
L103: Patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer that has progressed on first-line therapy have limited treatment options, and no standard-of-care therapy has been identified in the second-line setting.
L104: Current National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines for second-line treatment of recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer include pembrolizumab as a category 2A therapy option supported by a contemporary single-arm trial, and a host of chemotherapy agents as category 2B options supported by historical data developed before the adoption of
L105: chemotherapy doublet + bevacizumab in front line, which is considered the current standard of care (Marth 2017; Abu-Rustum 2020).
L106: The most commonly used agents in the US based on health claims data include pembrolizumab (for patients with tumors positive for programmed cell death ligand 1 [PD-L1]), liposomal doxorubicin, topotecan, vinorelbine, gemcitabine, or irinotecan as single agents (Kantar Health 2020).
L107: The data supporting the NCCN recommended therapies are almost entirely derived from trials conducted prior to the adoption of bevacizumab in combination with chemotherapy as first-line standard-of-care in 2014, or in some cases even prior to the adoption of platinum doublet therapy in the first-line setting in 2009.
L108: An exception is the KEYNOTE-158 trial of pembrolizumab (n=98) which led to accelerated approval in 2018 for the treatment of selected patients with PD-L1+ cervical cancer who have demonstrated disease progression on or after chemotherapy. The median duration of response (DOR) was not reached with median 10.2 months of follow up, and responses were seen in 14% of subjects with PD-L1+ tumors (Chung 2019). Median overall survival (OS) was 11 months in the PD-L1+ subgroup and 9.4 months for the entire trial population (Chung 2019). While patients who respond to pembrolizumab may potentially receive durable benefit, no responses were observed in subjects whose tumors did not express PD-L1, hence the large majority of second-line (and beyond) patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer do not benefit from treatment with pembrolizumab.
L109: Currently-used therapies and the trials supporting their inclusion in NCCN guidelines are described in Table 1. These trials generally had small sample sizes, often included unconfirmed response rates in the objective response rate (ORR) calculation, included adjuvant and neoadjuvant therapy as well as chemotherapy plus radiation as prior lines of therapy, allowed single-agent chemotherapy to be used as the standard-of-care in the front line, and included responses that were not assessed by IRC or by response evaluation criteria in solid tumors (RECIST). The efficacy of the chemotherapy agents is limited, with DORs of 6 months or less, and OS in the range of 6 to 9 months.

L121: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L122: Patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer have poor survival and no standard of care treatment options in the second line and beyond. With the exception of KEYNOTE-158, results from trials of currently-used therapies in the second line and beyond were conducted prior to the adoption of chemotherapy doublet plus bevacizumab as the standard of care first-line therapy. The trial results do not reflect the current US population being treated after first-line progression and thus it is difficult to extrapolate the data to current second-line treatment outcomes for patients. In addition, these trials generally had small sample sizes, often included unconfirmed response rates in the ORR calculation, and were not independently assessed by an IRC. These trials also do not have consistent eligibility criteria for first-line therapy: some trials allowed single agent chemotherapy to be used as the standard of care in the first line therapy, some included adjuvant and neoadjuvant therapy as well as chemotherapy plus radiation as prior lines of therapy before the utilization of single-agent chemotherapy, and one excluded patients subjects who that have received taxanes in the first line. Too few patients derive clinical benefit from these therapies, including pembrolizumab. Significant medical need remains for patients whose disease does not respond to or progress after first-line therapy.
L123: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L124: FDA agrees with the Applicant that current standard-of-care first-line treatment for patients in the U.S. with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer is a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab.
L125: In the Applicant's table of available therapies, FDA did not consider the following therapies to be available therapies for patients enrolled to GCT1015-04:
L126: - FDA does not consider single agent bevacizumab as an available 2L therapy for patients enrolled to GCT1015-04 as most patients enrolled to this trial had already received bevacizumab in combination with chemotherapy in the first line metastatic setting.
- FDA does not consider pembrolizumab as an available 2L therapy for patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer, as this product is under an accelerated approval and clinical benefit has not yet been confirmed.
- FDA does not consider fluorouracil or mitomycin+cisplatin as available 2L therapies, as the studies evaluating those products only enrolled patients in the 1L metastatic/recurrent setting, not in the 2L setting or beyond.
- FDA did not consider nab-paclitaxel as an available therapy for patients enrolled to GCT1015-04, as the nab-paclitaxel trial excluded patients who had received prior paclitaxel. All patients enrolled to GCT1015-04 had received prior paclitaxel.
L127: In the Applicant's table of available therapies, FDA has the following clarifications:
L128: - Docetaxel is listed as having an ORR of 8.7% without information on DOR. The median DOR was 3.8 months.
- Ifosfamide is listed in the table as having an ORR of 0%. In a separate study (not reported in the table), ifosfamide had an ORR of 11% (95% CI: 4.5, 24.8) with DOR range
L129: reported as 10-16 months (no median DOR reported) in the 2L setting (Sutton 1989).
L130: - In one study described in the table, irinotecan is listed as having an ORR=21% (95% CI: 10-35). The median DOR was 3 months, with no data on range available. FDA notes that in another study of irinotecan as 2L treatment, the ORR was 0% (Irvin 1998). It is not clear why there is a discrepancy in ORRs between these trials, as both were conducted in similar treatment settings.
- Pemetrexed is listed as having an ORR of 15% and the median DOR was 4.4. months, with no range available. In a separate trial, pemetrexed had an ORR of 12.9% and a median DOR of 1.75 months (Lorusso 2010).
L131: In summary, FDA disagrees with the Applicant that patients have no standard-of-care treatment options in the 2L setting and beyond, but FDA does think treatment options in this setting are limited. There are no 2L therapies for recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer that are approved under FDA regular approval but based on guideline recommendations standard of care available therapies include chemotherapies with response rates ranging from 5-21%. The median DOR ranges from 2-4 months. There are important caveats to this assessment. These data come from small studies and the efficacy of existing **2L** therapies after a 1L regimen with a chemotherapy doublet with bevacizumab is unknown. Bevacizumab was not considered standard-of-care treatment at the time that many trials described above were conducted. The review team expects that the efficacy (namely ORR) of several of the available single agent chemotherapies would be worse in the current 2L or later setting, following bevacizumab-containing doublet chemotherapy. In conclusion, FDA agrees that there is an unmet need for new treatments for patients with advanced, metastatic cervical cancer following first-line combination chemotherapy (+/- bevacizumab).

L144: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L145: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's assessment, with the following additions.
L146: At the Type B Pre-Phase 3 Meeting on April 29, 2019, FDA stressed the importance of ensuring that the confirmatory study is fully accrued or at least well underway at time of an accelerated approval action. At the August 31, 2020 meeting, FDA expressed concern that the proposed confirmatory study had not yet started enrolling patients. As the confirmatory study is enrolling patients in the same treatment line as the study supporting the accelerated approval, accrual could be challenging following an accelerated approval.
L147: FDA continued to express concerns regarding enrollment to the confirmatory trial, innovaTV 301, throughout review of this application. FDA and the Applicant had a Type C meeting on July 12, 2021 to discuss the status of the confirmatory trial. The Applicant had consented 18 patients and enrolled 8 patients. FDA recommended that the Applicant continue to make efforts to increase enrollment, including broadening eligibility criteria. FDA also stated that they would be requesting periodic updates regarding enrollment from the Applicant.
L148: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L149: ## 4.1 Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L150: Division of Oncology 1 consulted OSI to perform an audit of the Applicant and the overall trial conduct. FDA selected three sites (US11, US01, and BE01) for clinical inspection based on
L151: enrolling a large number of patients and/or higher tumor response rate compared to the ORR reported for GCT1015-04. The study sponsor, Genmab U.S., Inc. was also selected for inspection given that tisotumab vedotin is an NME and there have been no prior inspections of this sponsor. Based on these inspections, there were no significant regulatory violations, the trial conduct for GCT1015-04 appears adequate, and the submitted data from the three investigator sites appear reliable.
L152: Clinical inspection summaries are included here:
L153: ## **Site US11: Dr. Bradley Monk**
L154: Dr. Monk was inspected on May 3-7, 2021, as a surveillance and data audit for Study GCT1015-04. This was the first FDA inspection of Dr. Monk's conduct of clinical trials.
L155: The investigator site screened six subjects and enrolled four of them into the study. As of the data cutoff date of 02/06/2020, all the four subjects were discontinued from study treatment due to disease progression or AE(s) [i.e., severe or moderate ocular toxicities in Subjects (b) (6) - (b) (6) but remained on study for follow-up. As of the inspection, all the subjects were off study with death reported.
L156: All subjects' source records were reviewed during the inspection and were compared with the Applicant's submitted data for this site. The source records reviewed at the site included the informed consent forms, subject inclusion and exclusion criteria, prior chemotherapy, enrollment log, scans performed and subsequent submissions the IRC, AEs and serious AEs (SAE), protocol deviations, and other documents in subject study charts as well as subject data in electronic case report forms (eCRF). Regulatory records and study procedures were also reviewed, including the Institutional Review Board (IRB) approvals of all the protocol/amendments and related informed consents, site training documentation and updates during the study, FDA 1572s, delegation of responsibility, financial disclosures, access to the eCRF system, reports to the sponsor, study monitoring, study product control and accountability, and records retention.
L157: The inspection found no regulatory violations. All the enrolled subjects were eligible and were properly consented. The primary endpoint data and adverse events, including the reported ocular toxicities, were verifiable with source records at the site. All the scans were found to have been submitted to the IRC according to the Imaging Manual provided to the site. There was no evidence of underreporting AEs or protocol deviations.
L158: At the conclusion of this inspection, no Form FDA 483, Inspectional Observations, was issued to Dr. Monk.
L159: ## **Site US01: Dr. Michael Gold**
L160: Dr. Gold was inspected on June 8-9, 2021, as a data audit for Study GCT1015-04. For the investigator, this was the first FDA inspection. His site enrolled and treated two of the three screened subjects for the study. As of the data cutoff date, the two subjects were discontinued from study treatment due to disease progression. At the time of this inspection, both subjects had died.
L161: The inspection included data verification with source documentation and a review of the subject study charts, informed consents, eligibility, medical records, efficacy assessments, adverse events, case report forms, drug accountability, monitoring logs, study correspondences, regulatory documents (i.e., IRB approvals and communications, investigator's agreements, financial disclosures), training records, and protocol adherence.
L162: The inspection revealed no regulatory deficiencies, with no Form FDA 483 issued to Dr. Gold at the closeout. The enrolled subjects met the eligibility criteria and their efficacy and safety data, as submitted by the Applicant to the BLA, were verifiable with source documentation. Protocol deviations and adverse events were found to have been properly reported according to the protocol.
L163: ## **Site BE01: Dr. Ignace Vergote**
L164: Dr. Vergote was inspected between 5/24/2021 and 5/28/2021 as a data audit for Study GCT1015-04. This was the first FDA inspection of the foreign investigator. The site screened 10 subjects and enrolled 7 of them into the study. As of the data cutoff, all the seven subjects stopped study treatment, with six subjects secondary to disease progression and one (Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6)) due to severe ocular toxicities. At the time of this inspection, one subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) who started treatment on [REDACTED] (b) (6) was actively followed, with the last survival checked date [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L165: This inspection audited the reported efficacy and safety endpoint data and examined the Independent Ethics Committee (IEC) oversight of the study, protocol adherence, financial disclosures, enrollment log, informed consent and process, subject eligibility checklists and medical history records, scans performed and subsequent submission for central review, concomitant medications, adverse events, test article accountability and data entry to the electronic data capture system for the study.
L166: The inspection identified no significant regulatory violations, with no Form FDA 483 issued to Dr. Vergote. All the subjects were found to have met the eligibility criteria, consented prior to study-related procedures, and had the protocol-required assessments performed properly, including ophthalmology examinations at study entry and during the study. The submitted efficacy and safety data were confirmable with site's source records. Of note, there was no clear documentation of the reason that five additional vials of study product were dispensed for
L167: Subject (b) (6) while only four vials were needed for the intended dose. The inspection revealed that those five vials, including the initial shipped four vials and one replaced vial upon replacement, were noted to have particulates for which the five vials were discarded and destroyed accordingly.
L168: ## **Genmab U.S., Inc. (Trial Sponsor)**
L169: The study sponsor was inspected from 4/20/2021 through 4/28/2021 to evaluate the conduct and management of Study GCT1015-04. This was the first inspection of Genmab US Inc., a subsidiary of Genmab A/S.
L170: The inspection reviewed the study sponsor's history and its co-development of tisotumab vedotin with the current Applicant Seagen Inc., sponsor's responsibilities and activities in the study conduct, standard operating procedures, key individuals and their responsibilities, contract research organizations involved in the study and related agreements, selection of clinical investigators and monitors for the study and related training, financial disclosure forms, documentation of IRBs/IECs, monitoring plans, monitoring visit reports, adverse event reporting, protocol deviations, quality assurance procedures and activities, data collection and management as well as related meeting minutes.
L171: The inspection found no objectionable regulatory violations in the conduct of Study GCT1015-04, with no Form FDA-483 issued to Genmab US at the conclusion of this inspection. There were no study sites closed due to non-compliance. The sponsor's oversights of study monitoring, safety reporting, and data management were performed properly, with no deficiencies noted during the inspection. In addition, the study was confirmed to be ongoing as of the inspection but was closed to enrollment, with the last subject dosed with tisotumab vedotin (b) (6). At the closeout meeting, no discussion items were presented to the sponsor.
L172: ## 4.2 Product Quality
L173: The OPQ review team completed their review dated 8/24/21, signed by Kelly Ballard and Patrick Lynch. The review summarizes the quality assessment finding of the manufacturing information provided in the BLA submission and the review team did not find any approvability issues and have recommended approval. There are four PMCs related to the CMC review and they are described in section 14 of this review.
L174: ## 4.3 Clinical Microbiology
L175: As noted in Section 4.2, the OPQ team completed a combined review, inclusive of microbiology recommendations. The review team has recommended approval for this BLA.
L176: ## 4.4 Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
L177: Tisotumab vedotin is an anti-TF-targeted antibody-drug conjugate. The Applicant seeks an indication in patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer, regardless of tumor TF status. FDA agrees with this indication, and there is no need for a companion diagnostic to support this indication. For more details on TF analysis, refer to Sections 6.3.2 and 8.1.4.
L178: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L179: ## 5.1 Executive Summary
L180: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L181: Tisotumab vedotin (Tivdak) belongs to the pharmacological class of tissue factor (TF)-directed antibody and microtubule inhibitor conjugate, composed of a TF specific human IgG1κ antibody linked to the microtubule disrupting agent monomethyl auristatin E (MMAE) via a protease-cleavable maleimidocaproyl-valyl-citrullinyl-p-aminobenzoyloxycarbonyl (mc-val-cit-PABC) linker. The average drug to antibody ratio is approximately 4. Tisotumab vedotin is administered by intravenous infusion once every 3 weeks and is proposed for the treatment of adult patients with recurrent or metastatic cervical cancer with disease progression on or after chemotherapy at a recommended dose of 2 mg/kg. The nonclinical development program for tisotumab vedotin and the cytotoxic payload, MMAE, consisted of studies in mice, rats, and monkeys to evaluate the primary and safety pharmacology, general and developmental toxicology, and genotoxicity. During the nonclinical development, tisotumab vedotin was referred to as IgG1-1015-011-vcMMAE or HuMax-TF-ADC (IgG1-1015-011 or HuMax-TF for the unconjugated antibody, tisotumab) and MMAE as SGD-1010.
L182: Human tissue factor is a 43-47 kDa plasma membrane glycoprotein, consisting of an extracellular domain (aa 1-218), a hydrophobic transmembrane domain (aa 220-242), and a cytoplasmic domain (aa 243-263). Tissue factor is the main physiological initiator of the blood coagulation cascade. The extracellular domain binds to serine protease factor VII (FVII) to activate coagulation. The TF:FVIIa complex initiates the extrinsic pathway of blood coagulation by producing activated FX (FXa) and FIX (FIXa) resulting in production of thrombin and clot formation composed of insoluble fibrin and activated platelets. In addition to its role as a pro-coagulant activator, TF plays a role in both vasculogenesis in the developing animal and angiogenesis in normal and malignant adult tissues.
L183: In vitro, the unconjugated TF-directed antibody, tisotumab, bound TF with a KD of 3.15 nM;
L184: tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin bound to the recombinant extracellular domain of TF in a dose dependent manner with similar EC50 values of 2.5 nM (373 ng/mL) and 3.1 nM (469 ng/mL), respectively. Additionally, they bound similarly to TF expressing human cancer cells with EC50 values ranging from 0.19 nM (30 ng/mL) to 2.30 nM (346 ng/mL) depending on the cell line. The data suggests that conjugation of tisotumab does not affect the binding affinity to TF. Upon binding to TF on the cell membrane, tisotumab vedotin is internalized into the cell and localized to the lysosomes. In vitro, the extent of cytotoxicity depends on the level of TF cell surface expression; tisotumab vedotin IC50 values ranged from approximately 1.63 ng/mL to 575 ng/mL amongst the cell lines tested. Tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin induced antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) in a similar manner with EC50 values ranging from 0.031 nM (4.7 ng/mL) to 0.103 nM (15.4 ng/mL) at the conditions tested. Additionally, data suggests that tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin may have antibody-dependent cellular phagocytosis (ADCP) activity.
L185: In vivo, anti-tumor activity and/or tumor regression was seen in cervical cancer patient derived xenograft (PDX) models when mice were administered tisotumab vedotin at 4 mg/kg twice in the first week of study via intravenous dosing. Tisotumab vedotin or paclitaxel treated tumors that exhibited tumor re-growth (&gt;300 mm3) between days 42 and 60 showed regression following re-treatment with two doses of tisotumab vedotin. Intra-tumor hemorrhages were noted in 2/36 xenograft models (human pancreatic and lung adenocarcinoma) treated with tisotumab or tisotumab vedotin. Based on the data, it appears that one of the xenograft models (lung) is prone to hemorrhages in the absence of treatment; however, treatment with tisotumab vedotin enhanced the degree of hemorrhage observed.
L186: Secondary pharmacology studies to assess the effects of tisotumab on FXa generation and coagulation were evaluated using several in vitro assays. Generally, the assays showed that tisotumab can inhibit FXa and thrombin generation, delay clotting but not affect the strength of the formed clot in human plasma or whole blood. Similar results were seen with monkey plasma or whole blood when tested alongside human. In a non-GLP intra-animal dose escalation study in monkeys, a dose-dependent increase in bleeding time was noted 1-hour post dose; statistical significance was reached at the high dose (100 mg/kg, 55-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on Cmax) compared to the Day 1 vehicle dose. Hemorrhage in the heart and urinary bladder were noted in one female monkey at the end of the study. Bleeding/hemorrhage was an adverse event (AE) in patients with the most common being epistaxis, vaginal hemorrhage, and hematuria.
L187: The Applicant evaluated the effects of tisotumab vedotin on the nervous, cardiovascular, or respiratory systems in vitro and in animals. In vitro inhibition of the hERG channel was not evaluated with tisotumab vedotin; however, up to 100 µM MMAE had limited (&lt;50%) inhibition on hERG potassium channel. Additionally, tisotumab vedotin did not have effects on the nervous, cardiovascular, or respiratory systems in monkeys after repeated dosing up to 5 mg/kg
L188: (4.2-fold the exposures at the recommended dose based on AUC). Clinically, all grade peripheral neuropathy and grade 2 pneumonitis (leading to death in one patient) occurred in patients. Peripheral neuropathy is a commonly seen toxicity with antibody-drug conjugates with cytotoxic payloads but is generally not observed in nonclinical studies with the MMAE payload (Saber and Leighton 2015; Stagg et al. 2016). In a non-GLP dose range finding study in monkeys, one animal given 6 mg/kg tisotumab vedotin (4.9-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC) experienced respiratory symptoms described as gasping, wheezing, and shallow, labored breathing with macroscopic observation of discolored spongy lungs and microscopic findings of mild edematous congestion/hemorrhage 10 days after the first dose. Additionally, in the monkey GLP repeat dose toxicology study, two animals (1M, 1F) had microscopic findings of focal alveolar foamy macrophage accumulation at the high dose of 5 mg/kg (4.2-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
L189: In vitro, tisotumab vedotin was stable in human and monkey plasma with limited release of free MMAE over a two-week period, and the binding of MMAE to human plasma proteins ranged from 68 to 82%. In the 13-week GLP repeat-dose toxicology study after a single dose of tisotumab vedotin, the plasma exposure (C_{max} and AUC) increased in an approximately dose proportional manner with no apparent gender differences. The T_{max} was reached within 30 to 40 minutes post infusion, and the half-life was in the range of 1 to 2 days. The levels of free MMAE were minimal compared to those of tisotumab vedotin suggesting stability in plasma. Upon repeated infusions of tisotumab vedotin, no to minimal (\le 1.3-fold) accumulation was observed; T_{max} and the half-life maintained as compared to the single dose. After repeated dosing, animals developed anti-drug antibodies (ADA) especially at the high dose of 5 mg/kg, but ADA did not appear to affect the peak plasma concentration or total exposure. Animal to human exposure margins were calculated using the mean clinical AUC_{0-21} (57.5 day.\mug/mL) for tisotumab vedotin, C_{max} (40.8 ng/mL) for tisotumab, and C_{max} (5.91 ng/mL) and AUC_{0-21} (50 ng.day/mL) for MMAE after the first dose. Per the clinical pharmacology team, there was no accumulation with repeated dosing of tisotumab vedotin; therefore, the steady state exposures after repeated dosing were similar to exposures seen after the first dose.
L190: In monkeys receiving single intravenous doses up to 3 mg/kg radiolabeled tisotumab, &gt;10% tissue uptake of tisotumab was observed in the liver and bone marrow and &lt;10% tissue uptake was noted in the lungs, kidneys, heart, small intestine, and gall bladder; limited (\le 1.2\%) tissue uptake, but above background levels, was seen in the spleen, salivary gland, testes, and urinary bladder. In mice bearing TF-high or TF-low pancreatic tumors, the distribution of tisotumab vedotin and tisotumab were 23-fold and 5-fold higher, respectively, in TF-high compared to TF-low tumors while normal tissue distribution was generally comparable regardless of tumor type. MMAE was metabolized primarily by CYP3A4. MMAE is an inhibitor of CYP3A4 in vitro and a substrate for P-gp1. The major route of MMAE elimination was via feces in rats with similar observations in humans as 72% of MMAE was recovered in feces over a 1-week period.
L191: Tisotumab bound human and cynomolgus monkey TF similarly with EC50 values of 8.3 ng/mL and 6 ng/mL, respectively, with approximately 100% binding achieved at &lt;100 ng/mL. Tisotumab did not bind to mouse, rat, rabbit, pig, or dog TF. In a GLP-compliant tissue cross-reactivity study using tisotumab, membrane TF staining was generally similar across a variety of normal human and cynomolgus monkey tissues. Additionally, the plasma stability of tisotumab vedotin in pooled human and cynomolgus monkey plasma was similar with 99.3% and 99.5% of MMAE remaining conjugated to the antibody, respectively, up to 14 days. The data suggests that the cynomolgus monkey was the only relevant toxicological species.
L192: A GLP compliant repeat-dose toxicology study with tisotumab vedotin was conducted in monkeys (13-week), and the payload was assessed in rats (4-week) and monkeys (11-week) using the clinical intravenous route of administration. Toxicities that were TF specific and not observed in the rat and monkey studies assessing the payload included skin toxicities at ≥3 mg/kg (≥2.2-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC) leading to death from severe skin toxicity at 5 mg/kg (4.2-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). Additionally, ocular toxicity without microscopic correlates were noted at 5 mg/kg and 25 mg/kg tisotumab vedotin and tisotumab, respectively. Skin and ocular toxicities could be expected from the TF-directed antibody portion of tisotumab vedotin as tisotumab was shown to positively stain skin epithelial cells (squamous epidermis, hair follicle), and corneal and conjunctiva epithelial cells, in both human and monkey in a tissue cross reactivity study. Rash and ocular toxicity in the form of dry eye, and conjunctival or corneal toxicity with some cases of moderate to marked decreases in visual acuity were observed in patients.
L193: Toxicities that were likely MMAE mediated based on their presence in both the toxicology study with tisotumab vedotin and those with the payload only included decreased erythropoiesis, neutropenia, toxicities to the liver, bone marrow, lymphoid (thymus and/or spleen) and male reproductive (testes, epididymides) organs at comparable exposures as the recommended dose. In the monkeys treated with tisotumab vedotin at ≥3 mg/kg, animals received antibiotics due to marked neutropenia. All grade anemia and neutropenia (no neutropenic fever) were observed in patients including one patient death from neutropenic sepsis. Toxicology findings in rats (MMAE) and monkeys (tisotumab vedotin, MMAE) were reversible once dosing ceased except for findings in the male reproductive organs.
L194: Genetic toxicology studies were not conducted with tisotumab vedotin; rather, the applicant submitted studies that evaluated the payload. MMAE was negative in the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay and the mouse lymphoma forward mutation assay but was positive in the rat bone marrow micronucleus assay through an aneugenic mechanism. Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted with tisotumab vedotin in accordance with ICH S9 guidance.
L195: Dedicated fertility, early embryonic development, embryofetal development (EFD), pre- and postnatal development studies were not conducted with tisotumab vedotin in accordance with
L196: ICH S9 based on the expected adverse effects of MMAE on rapidly dividing cells and the intended indication for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer. The Applicant assessed the effects tisotumab vedotin on reproductive organs as part of the repeat-dose toxicology study in monkeys and provided results from an EFD study in rats evaluating the MMAE payload. Toxicity to the male reproductive organs (testes, epididymis) in monkeys were decreased testicular size and seminiferous tubule atrophy, reduction or absence in sperm count, and decreased sperm motility at doses \ge 1 mg/kg (\ge 0.6-fold exposures at the recommended dose based on AUC). There were no effects on the female reproductive organs or the duration of menstrual cycles in monkeys. MMAE was teratogenic and embryo lethal when administered to pregnant rats via two intravenous doses of 0.2 mg/kg during the period of organogenesis on gestational days 6 and 13. MMAE-related toxicities included post-implantation loss and external and skeletal malformations of fetuses at a dose of 0.2 mg/kg (\sim 0.5-fold the MMAE exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). Additionally, MMAE was present in the amniotic fluid and fetal serum suggesting placental transfer. The label advises females of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment and for 2 months after the last dose of tisotumab vedotin based on the recommendations for duration of contraception for genotoxic pharmaceuticals that are embryo-fetal toxic from the FDA guidance, "Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations". The guidance recommends a contraception period of 1 month with an additional 5 half-lives (tisotumab vedotin half-life = 4 days) for aneugenic pharmaceuticals for females. The label advises male patients with female partners of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment and for 4 months after the last dose based on the FDA guidance (3 months with an additional 5 half-lives of the drug). Additionally, the label advises lactating women not to breastfeed during treatment and for 3 weeks (5 half-lives of tisotumab vedotin) after the last dose due to the potential for severe adverse reactions in a breastfed child.
L197: ## **Recommendation:**
L198: The nonclinical data submitted to this BLA are adequate to support approval of Tivdak for the proposed indication.
L199: During the review cycle, the review team determined that a new pediatric study plan was needed to investigate Tivdak in pediatric tumors as TF is a relevant target in certain pediatric malignancies. A post-marketing requirement to conduct preclinical studies in pediatric-specific preclinical models to support the conduct of clinical investigations of Tivdak in pediatric tumors was communicated to the Applicant and Applicant agreed.
L200: ## 5.2 Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L201: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L202: N/A
L203: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L204: Tisotumab vedotin shares the same linker-drug conjugation as the marketed product Adcetris
L205: (brentuximab vedotin, BLA 125388). The Applicant provided original study reports for the assessment of the payload that have been previously submitted and reviewed under BLA 125388; the primary nonclinical review was referenced for these studies.
L206: ## 5.3 Pharmacology
L207: ## Primary pharmacology
L208: Original studies were submitted to support the mechanism of action of tisotumab vedotin and the unconjugated TF-directed antibody tisotumab. Key studies that support the label are summarized below.
L209: ## Binding Affinity
L210: **Study# (b) (4) 1015-083:** Binding of tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin to TF was evaluated using surface plasmon resonance, ELISA, and flow cytometry assays. Binding to TF via surface plasmon resonance demonstrated a K_D of 3.15 nM for tisotumab. Avidity studies using DNA shuffling between human and murine TF resulted in loss of tisotumab binding to TF when the human sequence encoding amino acids 42 to 84 was replaced by the murine sequence; tisotumab did not bind mouse TF. Tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin bound to the recombinant extracellular domain of TF in a dose dependent manner with similar EC_{50} values of 2.5 nM (373 ng/mL) and 3.1 nM (469 ng/mL), respectively. Additionally, membrane binding to TF expressing (&gt;300,000 TF molecules/cell) A431 epidermoid carcinoma, BXPC3 pancreatic adenocarcinoma, and MDA-MB-231 breast cancer cells resulted in EC_{50} values for tisotumab in the range of 0.19 nM (30 ng/mL) to 1.01 nM (150 ng/mL). As seen with binding to recombinant extracellular domain of TF, membrane binding of tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin to pancreatic adenocarcinoma cell line HPAF-II (&gt;350,000 TF molecules/cell) resulted in similar EC_{50} values of 2.08 nM (313 ng/mL) and 2.30 nM (346 ng/mL), respectively, suggesting that conjugation of the TF specific antibody does not affect the binding affinity.
L211: **Study# (b) (4) 1015-088:** Human TF shares 94.1%, 98.6%, 75.8%, 57.3%, and 56.4% amino acid sequence homology with TF from cynomolgus monkey, chimpanzee, dog, rat, and mouse, respectively. Cross-reactivity of tisotumab was evaluated using immunohistochemistry, flow cytometry, and ELISA assays. Tisotumab showed cross reactivity with cynomolgus TF by immunohistochemistry, but not with dog, pig or rabbit TF as evaluated using normal kidney tissues where TF is expressed in human renal corpuscles under normal physiological conditions; however, the conservation of TF expression in renal corpuscles across the different species was not confirmed. In a flow cytometry assay, tisotumab bound human embryonic kidney HEK293F cells expressing exogenous TF from human, chimpanzee, and cynomolgus monkey with similar EC_{50} values of 8.3 ng/mL, 6.8 ng/mL, and 6 ng/mL, respectively, with approximately 100% binding achieved at &lt;100 ng/mL. Tisotumab did not bind to HEK293F cells expressing exogenous mouse, rat, rabbit, pig, or dog TF. Additionally, tisotumab did not cross-react with recombinant mouse TF in an ELISA assay.
L212: ## *In Vitro Functional Activity*
L213: **Study# (b) (4) 1015-087:** In TF expressing SK-OV-3 (ovarian, 50,000-175,000 TF molecules/cell) and A431 (epidermoid carcinoma, &gt;300,000 TF molecules/cell) cells an average of approximately 50% of tisotumab co-localized with the lysosomal marker LAMP-1 after 16 hours and was degraded over time. Tisotumab vedotin induced cytotoxicity in a panel of TF expressing cancer cell lines; sensitivity to tisotumab vedotin was dependent on the level of cell membrane TF expression. In three cervical cancer cell lines expressing different levels of TF, IC50 values ranged from approximately 1.63 ng/mL to 575 ng/mL; tisotumab vedotin produced 87% cell kill at 10 ng/mL in the highest TF expressing and most sensitive cervical cancer (CaSki) cell line.
L214: **Study# (b) (4) 1015-084:** Tisotumab induced antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) with EC50 values ranging from 0.031 nM (4.7 ng/mL) to 0.103 nM (15.4 ng/mL) in three cancer cell lines co-cultured with PBMCs from three to six healthy donors; tisotumab vedotin was able to induce ADCC in a similar manner, suggesting that ADCC activity was preserved with conjugation.
L215: **Study# TRN-5631:** A concentration of 2 µg/mL tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin mediated antibody-dependent cellular phagocytosis (ADCP) with 41% to 93% and 38% to 72% activity, respectively, using BxPC3 or A431 target cells and one or two macrophage donors as effector cells. Baseline activity up to 26% and 34% was detected in the no drug and isotype control groups, respectively.
L216: ## *In Vivo Functional Activity*
L217: **Study# (b) (4) 1015-149:** The cervical squamous cell carcinoma CEXF773 PDX showed 25-50% TF positive high intensity (3+) staining in the tumor when evaluated via IHC. The tumors showed regression (tumor volume &lt;200 mm3) or complete response (no measurable tumor noted as 0 mm3) up to approximately day 42 when mice were given two intravenous doses (day 0, 7) of tisotumab vedotin at 4 mg/kg or three intravenous doses (day 0, 7, 14) of paclitaxel at 20 mg/kg. Then, 3/8 tisotumab vedotin treated and 4/8 paclitaxel treated tumors began to show re-growth and by day 60 had mean tumor volumes of 345 mm3 and 392 mm3, respectively. These animals were re-treated with 4 mg/kg HuMax-TF-ADC on Day 60 and 67. Re-treated tumors showed regression to ≤65 mm3 tumor volume between Day 63 and 84. One animal that received HuMax-TF-ADC during the main study and was re-treated was euthanized on day 70 during the re-treatment phase due to 22% body weight loss; weight loss was not otherwise observed in any of the groups.
L218: **Study# (b) (4) 1015-223:** In the CEXF633 PDX model, a statistically significant (p=0.007) decrease in mean tumor volume between tumors treated with tisotumab vedotin compared to the isotype control ADC was observed when mice were given two intravenous doses (day 0, 7) of tisotumab vedotin at 4 mg/kg. Based on the dot plot provided (no raw data available in the study report), 4/8 tumors did not appear to progress from the size at which they were
L219: randomized when treated with tisotumab vedotin. Tisotumab vedotin treatment effects were more pronounced in the CV1664 PDX model, where tisotumab vedotin treated tumors regressed over time with a statistically significant difference (p=0.0022) compared to the isotype control ADC when mice were given two intravenous doses (day 0, 7) of tisotumab vedotin at 4 mg/kg. According to the Applicant, although no data was presented, there were no minor effects on body weight in both PDX models.
L220: **Study#** (b) (4) **1015-102:** Intra-tumoral hemorrhages were observed in 1/5 xenograft models (BxPC-3 pancreatic adenocarcinoma) when treated with tisotumab at 50 mg/kg followed by 25 mg/kg weekly and in 1/31 xenograft models (NCI-H441 lung adenocarcinoma) when treated with tisotumab vedotin at 3 mg/kg twice weekly for 2 weeks or a single administration in the range of 0.5 to 4.5 mg/kg. In the NCI-H441 model, hemorrhages were seen in the absence of treatment as well but were enhanced with tisotumab vedotin treatment. Additionally, tumors treated with a cMet inhibitor were also observed to hemorrhage; therefore, it's likely that the tumor model itself is prone to hemorrhaging. In general, hemorrhages were restricted to the tumor area and did not appear to be dependent on the level of TF expressed by tumor cells, resolved spontaneously, and were not associated with clinical signs of illness or weight loss.
L221: ## Secondary Pharmacology data
L222: ***In vitro inhibition of activated factor X (FXa) Generation by Tisotumab:*** Tisotumab (\sim 6.6 nM, \sim 1 \mug/mL) inhibited FXa generation by 19% ((b) (4) 1015-086). Tisotumab vedotin was not tested.
L223: ***In vitro coagulation:*** The effects of tisotumab (unconjugated antibody) on outcomes related to clot formation were investigated in four in vitro studies: standard prothrombin time (PT), modified PT, thromboelastography (TEG), and thrombin generation assay (TGA) ((b) (4) 264005, (b) (4) 1015-086, HxTFADC N Study Report 0361, HxTFADC N Study Report 0364). Results show that tisotumab (10-20 \mug/mL) can increase time to clot formation and surrogate substrate generation when standard assay concentrations for TF are drastically reduced (\ge 1000-fold dilution). Tisotumab vedotin (up to 20 \mug/mL) was only tested in the standard PT study (no TF dilution) and no effect was observed on clotting time. Tisotumab did not affect the strength of clot formation.
L224: ***Skin Bleeding Time in Cynomolgus Monkey:*** A non-GLP intra-animal dose escalation study was conducted in cynomolgus monkeys to explore potential effects of tisotumab on coagulation and bleeding via a skin incision test ((b) (4) 514850). Tisotumab was administered as weekly doses of 0 (Phosphate Buffered Saline), 1, 10 and 100 mg/kg on days 1, 8, 15 and 22, respectively. There was no relationship between tisotumab dose level (1-100 mg/kg) and bleeding times. No effects on coagulation parameters were observed, and there were no toxicologically meaningful effects with supra-pharmacologic exposures.
L225: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L226: The translation of the in vitro coagulation outcomes to in vivo or humans is likely limited. The in vitro assays (PT, TEG, and TGA) were not designed or validated to investigate effects of
L227: functional inhibition of TF on coagulation initiation and clotting time in vitro. Positive controls for in vitro coagulation assays known to have a clinically relevant inhibition of TF do not exist. Furthermore, there were no toxicologically meaningful effects in cynomolgus monkey with supra-pharmacologic exposures of tisotumab (unconjugated antibody). Thus, any mechanistic potential for tisotumab vedotin to interfere with TF-dependent coagulation initiation is not anticipated to lead to adverse effects in vivo.
L228: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L229: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. However, in the in vivo skin bleeding time assessment in monkeys (Study# (b)(4)514850; reviewed in more detail under Section 5.5.1) bleeding time increased in a dose dependent manner by 13%, 65% and 93% at 1, 10, and 100 mg/kg, respectively, 1 hour after the infusion compared to the saline dosed Day 1 measurement, corresponding to approximately 0.6, 5, and 55-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on C_{max}. Additionally, minimal hemorrhage and myocardial degeneration in the heart and hemorrhage in the urinary bladder of one female was noted at necropsy.
L230: Data (presented by the FDA):
L231: **Study# (b)(4)1015-086:** Tisotumab inhibited TF:FVIIa induced FXa generation by 19% at the highest concentration of 0.9 µg/mL. Tisotumab led to delayed clotting by 2.5-fold at the highest concentration of 10 µg/mL; however, it did not inhibit clot formation.
L232: **Study# (b)(4)264005:** In the standard PT assay, neither tisotumab or tisotumab vedotin up to 20 µg/mL had an effect on prothrombin time in human or monkey pooled plasma (single experiment). In the diluted (x3000) PT assay, a 1.4-fold increase in PT was seen with both tisotumab and tisotumab vedotin at 20 µg/mL compared to the control in human plasma; the variation between replicates was greater than those seen in the standard PT assay. In monkey plasma, there appears to be a trend towards increased PT; however, the variation between replicates in the control sample were too large to reliably compare to the treatment groups. In the 13-week repeat-dose monkey toxicology study, a 10-20% increase in PT at an exposure (C_{max}) &gt;8-fold higher than in this assay (100 µg/mL) was observed; no change in PT was observed with tisotumab vedotin near the highest dose (100 µg/mL) tested in this assay. Per the Applicant, the diluted assay was run for the first time and was not optimized; therefore, the results may not accurately reflect those that may be obtained had the assay been optimized.
L233: **Study# HxTFADC-N-Study-Report-0361:** Tisotumab increased time to clot and clot formation time in a dose dependent manner up to 2.6-fold and 1.6-fold, respectively, compared to control in human whole blood (single donor) at 20 µg/mL in the rotational thermoelectrometry assay; the maximal clot formation was not affected by tisotumab. Tisotumab delayed thrombin generation in plasma; the lag time increased by 15-fold, and thrombin activity decreased by 20-fold at 20 µg/mL compared to control in the calibrated automated thrombography (CAT) assay.
L234: **Study# HxTFADC-N-Study-Report-0364:** In the TEG assay, tisotumab prolonged clotting time by
L235: an average of 1.8-fold in human whole blood (n=3 donors) and by 2.5-fold in monkey whole blood (n=3 donors) at 20 µg/mL corresponding to an average of 21% and 80% inhibition of TF activity, respectively. The clot strength was not affected by tisotumab in either human or monkey. In the TGA assay, tisotumab led to an increase in lag time by an average of 2.3-fold in human and 3.7-fold in monkey plasma at 20 µg/mL.
L236: ## Safety Pharmacology
L237: Data (presented by the Applicant):
L238: Limited inhibition (&lt;50%) of potassium conductance via the human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) channel was observed with the highest concentration of monomethyl auristatin E (MMAE) (100 µM, 71.8 µg/mL), which was ~12,000-fold higher than the clinically observed Cmax of MMAE. At 10 µM MMAE (7.18 µg/mL) there was no significant inhibition of hERG channel activity. No effect was observed on the Fridericia corrected QT interval and RR-interval in cynomolgus monkeys treated with MMAE. Tisotumab vedotin was not evaluated in the hERG channel assay as the antibody component is too large to cross plasma membranes and therefore would be unable to access and block the promiscuous inner pore of the hERG channel.
L239: No effect was observed on electrocardiogram (ECG), heart rate, blood pressure, respiratory, or central nervous system (CNS) safety pharmacology parameters evaluated as part of the general toxicology studies performed in cynomolgus monkeys with tisotumab vedotin and tisotumab.
L240: ## The Applicant's Position:
L241: Tisotumab vedotin has no effects on safety pharmacology endpoints. In addition, there was no evidence of clinical effects relating to safety pharmacology endpoints. No effect was observed on ECG, heart rate, blood pressure, respiratory or CNS safety pharmacology parameters evaluated as part of the general toxicology studies performed in cynomolgus monkeys with the antibody-drug conjugate (ADC). No effect was observed on cynomolgus monkeys at physiological concentrations of MMAE. It is unlikely that MMAE derived from tisotumab vedotin would block hERG K+ channels in vivo.
L242: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L243: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. There were no tisotumab vedotin related effects on the safety pharmacology evaluations of the nervous, cardiovascular or respiratory systems at doses up to 5 mg/kg (4.2-fold the exposures at the recommended dose based on AUC) (Study# (b)(4) 522531) nor MMAE-related effects at a dose of 0.058 mg/kg (exposures similar to those at the recommended dose based on Cmax) (Study# (b)(4) 163.16). However, in a non-GLP dose range finding study in monkeys (Study# (b)(4) 521988), one animal given 6 mg/kg tisotumab vedotin (4.9-fold the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC) experienced respiratory symptoms described as gasping, wheezing, shallow, and labored breathing that led to early euthanasia.
L244: ## 5.4 ADME/PK
L245: ## The Applicant's Position:
L246: Following the first dose of tisotumab vedotin in cynomolgus monkeys, tisotumab vedotin exhibited a limited volume of distribution and an apparent terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) of 1 to 2 days. Tisotumab vedotin exposure over a dose-range of 1-6 mg/kg was approximately dose proportional after the first dose. Molar concentrations of MMAE were approximately 0.01% of peak tisotumab vedotin concentrations, confirming a high stability of tisotumab vedotin in the systemic circulation. In some animals that had high ADA titers, a decrease in tisotumab vedotin and an increase in unconjugated MMAE in circulation were observed upon subsequent dose administrations. However, in general, tisotumab vedotin exposures were sufficiently maintained during the treatment period even in the presence of ADA, particularly at the high dose (5 mg/kg). Since tisotumab vedotin shares the same linker-drug conjugation of the marketed product Adcetris® (brentuximab vedotin) and Padcev™ (enfortumab vedotin), historical data on the distribution, metabolism, excretion, and drug interaction potential of MMAE are applicable and discussed. MMAE was identified as a substrate and a metabolism-based inhibitor of CYP3A4 in vitro. In humans, coadministration of brentuximab vedotin with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor (ketoconazole) increased MMAE exposures by 34%, and a CYP3A4 inducer (rifampin) decreased MMAE exposures by 46% (Han 2013), but coadministration of brentuximab vedotin did not affect exposure to midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A substrate (Han 2013). Results from in vitro studies suggest that MMAE is a substrate of P-gp but not a substrate of BCRP, MRP2, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3. Furthermore, MMAE is not an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, BSEP, MRP2, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3 at clinically relevant concentrations.
L247: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L248: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. See the clinical pharmacology Section 6 for information regarding human data and drug interaction studies.
L249: ## Data (presented by the FDA):

L251: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761208}
## {tisotumab vedotin}
L252: Type of Study	Major Findings
Distribution
Determination of Whole Body Distribution of IV Bolus [^{89}\text{Zr}-89]-Df-IAb456_FL by Positron Emission Tomography (PET) (Study# KVU0001)	Monkey
Following a single intravenous infusion of positron emitting radionuclide Zirconium-89 (^{89}\text{Zr}) labelled and iron chelating agent deferoxamine conjugated tisotumab at 0.4, 1, and 3 mg/kg (n=1/sex/dose):Highest tissue uptakes were observed in the liver (21%), bone marrow (19%), lungs (9.5%), kidneys (4.2%), heart (11%), small intestine (5.9%), and gall bladder (1.7%). Tissue uptake that was above background activity but \le 1.2\% was seen in spleen, salivary gland, testes and urinary bladder.
There was no apparent difference in the tissue uptake between males and females or the different doses tested.

Biodistribution of ^{89}\text{Zr}-DfIAb37_FL and ^{89}\text{Zr}-Df-IAb37_FL in a high TF-expressing pancreas cancer xenograft model (Study# IMA001)	Mouse
Following a single intravenous injection of positron emitting radionuclide Zirconium-89 (^{89}\text{Zr}) labelled and iron chelating agent deferoxamine conjugated tisotumab or tisotumab vedotin at 1 mg/kg to TF-high pancreatic (HPAF-II; n=6/group) or TF-no/low pancreatic (PANC-I; n=3/group) tumor bearing SCID mice:Higher distribution of both tisotumab vedotin (23-fold) and tisotumab (5-fold) in TF-high tumors compared to TF-low tumors (6 days post dose).
Generally similar distribution of tisotumab vedotin and tisotumab in normal tissues of both TF-high and TF-low tumor bearing mice, except:A higher distribution of tisotumab vedotin in liver (>3-fold) and kidney (\sim 2-fold) compared to tisotumab in both TF-high and TF-low tumor bearing mice.
In the blood and spleen, distribution was higher in mice with TF-low bearing tumors.


The study evaluating the payload, MMAE, was previously reviewed by the FDA under the Applicant's BLA 125388 (brentuximab vedotin). The major findings from the primary review were included below.
Plasma Protein Binding Assay of MMAE by Ultracentrifugation (Study# XS-0025)	[\text{}^3\text{H}]\text{-MMAE} at 1, 10, and 100 nM was added to plasma of ICR mouse, Wistar rat, cynomolgus monkey, and human. Radioactivity in plasma and supernatant was measured by a liquid scintillation counter (LSC).The [\text{}^3\text{H}]\text{-MMAE} plasma protein binding ratio was 19 to 29% in mouse, 72 to 74% in rat, 17 to 19% in monkey, and 68 to 82% in human.
The plasma protein binding of [\text{}^3\text{H}]\text{-MMAE} was species-dependent.
There was no change in binding from 1 to 100 nM with rat and monkey plasma, but small differences were observed with mouse and human plasma.

Metabolism	
The metabolism studies with MMAE provided by the Applicant were previously reviewed by the FDA under the Applicant's BLA 125388 (brentuximab vedotin). The major findings from the primary review were included below.
Reaction Phenotyping: Identification of Human CYP Enzymes Involved in the In Vitro Metabolism of [\text{}^3\text{H}]\text{-MMAE} (Study# XT084006)	[\text{}^3\text{H}]\text{-MMAE} was converted to 8 radioactive compounds by NADPH-fortified human liver microsomes.
The major metabolites identified by high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) retention time (min) were 7.0 (by O-demethylation), 8.6 (by N-demethylation), and 11.1 (by dehydrogenation).
The formation of these major metabolites was primarily mediated by CYP3A4. Additional CYP450 enzymes (i.e. CYP2D6) might be minor contributors to the formation of these metabolites.
L253: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761208}
## {tisotumab vedotin}
L254: Type of Study	Major Findings
Metabolite Characterization of [3H]-MMAE in Rat, Monkey, and Human Hepatocytes (Study# XT084007)	The extent of 10 µM [3H]-MMAE metabolism by rat, monkey, and human hepatocytes was analyzed by liquid chromatography mass spectrometry (LC/MS/MS) over 240 minutes.
Up to 32% (in rat), 18% (in monkey), and 31% (in human) loss of [3H]-MMAE in hepatocytes was reported. The rank order (greatest to least) of observed substrate loss over the 240-minute incubations was rat ≈ human > monkey.
12 metabolites formed by hydroxylation, demethylation, dehydrogenation or hydrolysis were detected following incubation of 10 µM [3H]-MMAE with hepatocytes.
All 12 metabolites were detected from monkey hepatocytes, two of which were specific to the monkey (C2, C11) and one of which was specific to rat and monkey (C1). According to the Applicant, two metabolites, C4 and C6, formed by O-demethylation and hydroxylation, respectively, could be major metabolites in all three species.

Excretion	
The excretion study provided by the Applicant was previously reviewed by the FDA under the Applicant's BLA 125388 (brentuximab vedotin). The major findings from the primary review for [3H]-MMAE were included below.

Excretion, Mass Balance and Pharmacokinetics of Radioactivity in Sprague-Dawley Rats Following a Single Intravenous Bolus Dose of cAC10-vc-3H-MMAE or [3H]-MMAE (Study# 420501)	The major route of MMAE elimination in rats was via feces, suggesting biliary excretion with some recovery in urine.
Approximately 97% (males) or 102% (females) of the radiolabeled material was detected in feces. Approximately 15% (males) or 9% (females) of the radiolabeled materials was detected in urine.
No radioactivity was recovered in the carcass (with [3H]-MMAE) at 28 days post dose, suggesting that elimination was complete at this time after a single dose administration.
In the 1st 48 hours post-dose, approximately 90-95% [3H]-MMAE was excreted during this period. The half-life of [3H]-MMAE was 1-2.3 days. It is expected that excretion of tisotumab vedotin will be slower compared to unconjugated MMAE due to higher plasma stability and longer half-life of tisotumab vedotin compared to free MMAE.
The majority of radiolabeled components seen in urine and feces was identified as [3H]-MMAE rather than the parent ADC.

Other	
In vitro stability assessment of HuMax-TF-ADC (Study# SR1015-109-01)	
Plasma (pooled mouse, cynomolgus monkey, human) and PBS with 1% bovine serum albumin were incubated with 50 µg/mL HuMax-TF-ADC up to 14 days at 37°C.
Release of free MMAE in PBS/1%BSA, mouse, monkey, and human over the two-week period was 0.2%, 20%, 0.5%, and 0.7%, respectively.

L257: ## 5.5 Toxicology
L258: ## 5.5.1 General Toxicology
L259: Data (presented by the FDA):
L260: The 3-month GLP repeat dose toxicology study in the monkey was initially reviewed by the FDA under IND (b) (4) and findings are presented as in the original IND or reformatted for the BLA review.
L261: ## **Study title/number: HuMax-TF-ADC: 13 Week Repeat Dose Toxicity Study in Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 6 Week Recovery Period/522531**
L262: - The major clinical signs were skin toxicity (dry, red, flaky skin and skin sloughing) at \ge 3 mg/kg and ocular toxicity at 5 mg/kg HuMax-TF-ADC and 25 mg/kg HuMax-TF without microscopic correlates.
- A decrease in erythropoiesis (hemoglobin, red blood cells, and hematocrit), total leukocytes, neutrophils, lymphocytes, eosinophils, and basophils at \ge 4 mg/kg; animals received antibiotics throughout the study due to neutropenia.
- Major target organs included the spleen and testis/epididymis at \ge 1 mg/kg, bone marrow and skin at \ge 3 mg/kg, and the eye at 5 mg/kg. All findings were reversible except in the testes/epididymides and spleen.
L263: GLP compliance: Yes
L264: ## **Methods**
L265: Dose:	HuMax-TF-ADC
Main study: 0, 1, 3, and 5 mg/kg

HuMax-TF

Route of administration:	Intravenous infusion
Formulation/Vehicle:	Sodium chloride 0.9% w/v, USP

Recovery: 2/sex/group
Age:	4-6 years

In the interim study (Group 6), animals were treated with a single dose of 4 mg/kg HuMax-TF-ADC on Day 1 and sacrificed on Day 12 to better evaluate drug-related skin reactions.
Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results:	No
L266: ## **Observations and Results: Changes from control**
L267: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	One female given 4 mg/kg (interim study) was euthanized on Day 11; one male and two females given 5 mg/kg were euthanized on Day 10, 26, and 41, respectively. The cause of death was due to adverse skin reactions (noted below for clinical signs).
Clinical Signs	
Observations for both 3 and 5 mg/kg groups were generally noted by day 6. Animals dosed at 3 mg/kg generally recovered prior to the second dose, and the observations did not recur with subsequent dosing. Observations in the animals dosed at 5 mg/kg generally persisted or recurred with subsequent dosing. The clinical signs fully recovered by the end of the recovery period.
