L1615: **ADC Model:** The ADC plasma concentration-time data is fitted with a two-compartment model with parallel linear and Michaelis-Menten (MM) elimination (V_{max}, K_m). Baseline covariate effects of special interest: body weight on CL, V_1, V_2, and Q; albumin and sex on CL, V_1 and V_2; age, ECOG, hepatic impairment, eGFR, tumor size, and tumor type on CL; ECOG, hepatic impairment, and eGFR on V_1 were studied in the development of the ADC covariate model. All other covariates including race were tested by diagnostic plots of random effects versus covariates. No dependencies on race were noticeable to be clinically meaningful. The effects of body weight on CL, V_1, V_2, and Q, and the effects of albumin and sex on CL and V_1 were included in the final ADC model. None of the other covariates, including ADA status, drug material, and TFE H-score, have a clinically meaningful impact on ADC exposures. The final ADC model parameter estimates are summarized in Table 47. The covariate effects are summarized in Table
L1616: 48. The final ADC model was incorporated into the combined ADC-MMAE model. The final ADC model was further assessed in the combined model.
L1617: **ADC-MMAE Model:** The structure of base ADC-MMAE Model is shown in Figure 14. 100% of the linear ADC output was directed to the delay MMAE compartment while the remaining fractions were estimated. The effects of weight, sex, age, albumin, tumor size and type, ECOG score, hepatic impairment, and eGFR on MMAE clearance and weight, sex, age, albumin, ECOG score, hepatic impairment, and eGFR on MMAE volume were investigated in addition to the covariate effects on ADC PK parameters that were included in the final ADC model. All other covariates including race were evaluated by diagnostic plots of random effects versus covariates. No dependencies of clearance on race were noticeable to be clinically meaningful. Dependence of the delay rate constant on age and weight were also studied. The effects of age and sex on MMAE clearance and sex, eGFR, and hepatic impairment on MMAE volume were removed from the final ADC-MMAE model. The estimates of fixed-effect parameters of the final ADC-MMAE model, covariate fixed-effect parameters and variance parameters are shown in Table 48, Table 49 and Table 50. The covariate effects are summarized in Table 51. The final ADC-MMAE model was assessed via goodness-of-fit indicators and prediction performance (e.g., plots, see Figure 16 and Figure 17).

L1646: **Covariate Effects:** The effects of covariates on steady-state exposure (in Cycle 6) is illustrated in Figure 18. ADC exposure was most influenced by weight, with higher exposure in heavier patients. MMAE exposure depended on 9 covariates. Tumor size had the strongest effect, with higher exposure in patients with higher tumor size. Several other covariates that are generally correlated with overall patients' condition (albumin, ECOG, decrease of eGFR and hepatic impairment) also increased MMAE exposure. The combined effect of these covariates may more than double the exposure in patients with poor overall health status compared to exposure of the reference (healthier) subject. This is consistent with the high value of the upper bound of 90% prediction interval of MMAE exposure in the study population.
L1647: Figure 18. Effects of Covariates on Steady-state Exposure (in Cycle 6).
L1648: Source: Figures 8,9,10, and 11 in the population PK report.
L1649: **Impact of Hepatic Impairment on ADC and MMAE Exposure:** As shown in Figure 19, precited ADC exposure for patients with mild hepatic impairment based on NCI organ dysfunction working group (NCI-ODWG) is similar to patients with normal hepatic function, while MMAE exposures are 37% higher. This
L1650: is in agreement with the population PK model that included the effect of hepatic impairment on MMAE clearance.

L1660: ## Exposure Response Analyses:
L1661: **Exposure Metrics:** The population PK analysis showed that there was no appreciable accumulation of ADC and MMAE with repeated dosing. Therefore, Cycle 1 exposure measures were used for all exposure-response analyses. Cycle 1 was defined as the interval between the first and the second doses. Moreover, trough concentrations of ADC were either below quantification limit (BQL) or close to it. Therefore, ADC trough (minimum) concentration was not a useful measure of exposure. Thus, the individual estimates of the following exposure measures in Cycle 1 were used for the exposure-response analyses in addition to average ADC concentration from time zero to the end of the cycle in which event occurred (CavgLast):
L1662: - For ADC: AUC (AUC1, ADC) and Cmax (Cmax1, ADC);
- For MMAE: AUC1, MMAE, Cmax1, MMAE, and Ctrough (Ctrough1, MMAE).
L1663: **Data:** The database for the exposure-safety analysis included the data from 272 subjects administered TV 2.0 mg/kg 1Q3W doses in studies GEN701 (expansion cohort), GEN702 (expansion cohort), and GCT1015-04 (cutoff date February 6, 2020). The database for the exposure-efficacy analysis included the data from 101 subjects from Study GCT1015-04 (cutoff date February 6, 2020).
L1664: **Exposure Safety Analyses:** Treatment-emergent adverse events (AEs) investigated include treatment related AEs, All SAEs, treatment-related Grade 3+ TEAEs, treatment-related TEAEs leading to dose interruption, treatment-related TEAEs leading to dose reduction, treatment-related TEAEs leading to dose discontinuation, Grade 2+ bleeding TEAEs, Grade 2+ ocular TEAEs, and Grade 2+ peripheral neuropathy. For each TEAE type, linear logistic regression base models were implemented to assess the relationship between the probability of TEAE occurrence and exposure. To define the confidence interval for the logistic regression function, 1000 bootstrap samples were drawn with replacement from the analysis population, and the logistic regression base model was fitted to each of these samples. For each exposure value, 95% confidence interval of the logistic regression curve was defined as the 5th and 95th percentiles of the model predictions among 1000 bootstrap data sets. A significance level of \alpha = 0.05 was used for evaluation of the exposure coefficient of the base model (slope parameter). For each TEAE type, a covariate analysis was conducted using the full model approach. The full model had the following structure: \text{logit}(\text{EVENT}) \sim \text{Intercept} + \text{Exposure} + \text{AGE} + \text{WT} + \text{SUMDIAM} + \text{ALB} + \text{LDH} + \text{TFE} + \text{factor}(\text{SEXF}) + \text{factor}(\text{USA}) + \text{factor}(\text{CERVICAL}) + \text{factor}(\text{RENAL2}) + \text{factor}(\text{RENAL3}) + \text{factor}(\text{HEP}) + \text{factor}(\text{ECOG}), where *Exposure* was represented by one of the investigated ADC or MMAE exposure
L1665: measures, AGE is individual age at baseline (years), WT is individual body weight at baseline (kg), TFE is individual membrane tissue factor expression (H-score) at baseline, SUMDIAM is individual sum of tumor diameters at baseline (mm), ALB is individual albumin concentration at baseline (g/L), LDH is individual LDH concentration at baseline (U/L), SEXF is equal to 1 for females and 0 for males, USA is equal to 1 for patients from USA and 0 otherwise, CERVICAL is 1 for patients with cervical cancer, RENAL2 is 1 for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment, RENAL3 is 1 for patients with moderate renal impairment; HEP is 1 for patients with mild hepatic impairment, and ECOG is the ECOG score (0 or 1). The main goal of the covariate analysis was to confirm association of exposure with response after adjustment for covariates. Therefore, specific covariate results are not discussed. A significance level of \alpha = 0.05 was used for evaluation of the exposure coefficient of the covariate model (slope parameter). The main safety results are summarized in Table 52.
L1666: Table 52. Summary of Main Exposure Safety Analyses Results.

L1675: Agency asked the sponsor to update the ER for safety endpoints on ocular toxicity defined by the terms: Asthenopia, Chalazion, Chorioretinopathy, Conjunctival abrasion, Conjunctivitis allergic, Corneal epithelium defect, Corneal bleeding, Corneal erosion, Corneal irritation, Corneal toxicity, Diplopia, Erythema of eyelid, Eyelids pruritus, Eye discharge, Eye movement disorder, Eye pruritus, Foreign body sensation in eyes, Herpes ophthalmic, Keratopathy, Meibomian gland dysfunction, Ocular hypertension, Open angle glaucoma, Periorbital oedema, Retinal exudates. In case no incidence of any the following terms, no action is needed. In addition, covariates such as ‘history of dry eye’ if available should be considered. The sponsor responded on 5/10/2021 as follows:
L1676: - The Agency's requested preferred terms of Asthenopia, Chalazion, Chorioretinopathy, Conjunctival abrasion, Conjunctivitis allergic, Corneal epithelium defect, Corneal bleeding, Corneal erosion, Corneal irritation, Corneal toxicity, Diplopia, Erythema of eyelid, Eyelids pruritus, Eye discharge, Eye movement disorder, Eye pruritus, Foreign body sensation in eyes, Herpes ophthalmic, Keratopathy, Meibomian gland dysfunction, Ocular hypertension, Open angle glaucoma, Periorbital oedema, and Retinal exudates were included in the ocular AE
L1677: specifications. However, there were no grade 2+ ocular AEs with these terms reported in the BLA dataset. Thus, the dataset for analysis remained the same.
L1678: - Two additional covariates were considered in the updated exposure response (ER) safety analysis for ocular toxicities: 1) history of dry eye and 2) any pre-existing ocular AE at baseline. Inclusion of these covariates (history of dry eye or any ocular AE at baseline) did not alter the relationships of ocular AEs for any grade or for grade 2+ with exposure.
L1679: The requested analysis yielded the same conclusion for the ocular ER safety analysis as previously submitted in the BLA. An additional ER safety analysis has been conducted for the all grade AEs of special interest (AESIs; ocular, bleeding, and peripheral neuropathy). The trends above are identical to the ER safety analysis for grade 2+ AESIs, and thus the overall conclusions remain the same.
L1680: **Exposure Efficacy Analyses:** The overall response rate (ORR) was investigated using logistic regression models. The linear logistic models were implemented to assess the relationships between the probability of ORR and exposure measures. A covariate analysis was conducted using the full model approach. Categorical covariates with less than 10% representation were not included in the full model. The full models had the following structure: \text{logit}(\text{ORR}) \sim \text{Intercept} + \text{Exposure} + \text{AGE} + \text{WT} + \text{SUMDIAM} + \text{ALB} + \text{LDH} + \text{TFE} + \text{factor}(\text{RENAL2}) + \text{factor}(\text{RENAL3}) + \text{factor}(\text{ECOG}) + \text{factor}(\text{SQUAM}) + \text{factor}(\text{RADI2}) + \text{factor}(\text{BEVA}), where SQUAM is 1 for patients with squamous cancer, RADI2 is 1 for patients with prior radiation therapy, BEVA is 1 for patients with prior bevacizumab therapy.
L1681: The relationships between exposure and TTE were first characterized using base Cox proportional hazard (CPH) models that describe the marginal effect of exposure on survival without consideration of covariates. Linear dependence of \text{log}(\text{hazard}) on exposure was tested. A significance level of \alpha = 0.05 was used for model selection and for evaluation of the exposure coefficients of the selected models. The covariates were added linearly in the log hazard scale as follows:
L1682: \text{log}(\text{hazard}) \sim \text{Exposure} + \text{AGE} + \text{WT} + \text{SUMDIAM} + \text{ALB} + \text{LDH} + \text{TFE} + \text{factor}(\text{RENAL2}) + \text{factor}(\text{RENAL3}) + \text{factor}(\text{ECOG}) + \text{factor}(\text{SQUAM}) + \text{factor}(\text{RADI2}) + \text{factor}(\text{BEVA}).
L1683: Covariate analysis was not conducted for DOR as DOR analysis data included only 24 patients, not sufficient for a meaningful covariate analysis. The main exposure efficacy analyses results are summarized in Table 53. For all significant exposure-efficacy relationships, the relationship with MMAE exposure was opposite to the relationship with ADC exposure even though there were no strong correlations between ADC exposure measures and MMAE exposure measures. Note that the ER relationships have not been fully characterized given the data included in ER analyses were from studies at one dose level.
L1684: Table 53. Summary of Main Efficacy Exposure Response Analyses Results.
L1685: For the base models, probability of response significantly increased with increase of ADC exposure (Cycle 1 AUC and Cycle 1 Cmax). After accounting for the covariate effects, only the relationship with ADC Cycle 1 Cmax remained statistically significant.

L1694: We agree with sponsor's conclusions and rationale on inclusion of each of the covariates in the respective full final model as following:
L1695: - Demographic factors (age and weight), laboratory assessments (baseline albumin, baseline LDH) and subject health status (baseline tumor size, renal impairment, hepatic impairment, and baseline ECOG performance) are important prognostic factors for cervical and other cancers.
- Race has been identified as an important prognostic factor in cervical cancer.
- Histology (squamous/not) has been identified as an important prognostic factor in cervical cancer.
- Prior bevacizumab treatment and radiation therapy have a high probability of being employed in earlier lines (1L+) of cervical cancer therapy and have been identified as important prognostic factors in cervical cancer.
- Region (US versus Europe) captures potential geographic differences in subject care or environment.
- Tumor type captures potential differences between cervical cancer and the other pooled tumor types.
- Sex captures potential gender-related differences across cancer types.
- Baseline tumor tissue factor H-score is directly relevant to the mechanism of action of tisotumab vedotin. Differences in tumor tissue factor expression between subjects might confound the exposure response assessment.

Not applicable.

L1727: Diagram illustrating the ADC-MMMAE model structure and parameter estimates.
The model consists of four compartments: Peripheral ADC, Central ADC, Delay MMMAE, and Central MMMAE.
Peripheral ADC (Q, V_2) is connected to Central ADC (CL, V_1, V_{MAX}, K_M) via inter-compartment clearance Q/V_2 (downward flow) and Q/V_1 (upward flow).
Central ADC receives the ADC IV Dose input. Elimination from Central ADC occurs via:
Non-specific elimination: CL/V_1 (downward flow).
Target-mediated elimination: V_{MAX} / (K_M + ADC) (downward flow).
The non-specific elimination flux CL/V_1 splits into:
Flux to Delay MMMAE: DAR \cdot CL / V_1. DAR is defined as DAR = 1 + 3 \cdot \exp(-\beta \cdot \text{tad}).
Flux to Central MMMAE: FR_1 \cdot DAR \cdot CL / V_1.
The target-mediated elimination flux from Central ADC (V_{MAX} / (K_M + ADC)) splits into:
Flux to Delay MMMAE: FR_2 \cdot DAR \cdot V_{MAX} / (K_M + ADC).
Flux to Central MMMAE: CL_{MMMAE} / V_{MMMAE}.
Flux from Delay MMMAE to Central MMMAE is governed by the rate constant k_{tr}.
Central MMMAE (CL_{MMMAE}, V_{MMMAE}) is the terminal compartment, with elimination flux CL_{MMMAE} / V_{MMMAE}.
Legend:
CL: ADC clearance; Q: ADC inter-compartment clearance; V1: ADC central volume; V2: ADC peripheral volume; Vmax: maximum ADC Michaelis-Menten elimination rate; KM: Michaelis-Menten constant of ADC elimination.
CLMMMAE: MMAE apparent clearance; VMMMAE: MMAE apparent central volume. FR1: fraction of ADC non-specific elimination that goes to the central compartment; FR2: fraction of ADC target-mediated elimination that goes to the delay compartment; ktr: delay compartment rate constant;
DAR: drug to antibody ratio assumed to follow an exponential decay from the initial value of 4 to the lowest value of 1; \beta: rate constant of DAR decline with time after dose; tad: time after dose.

Model 137 type_3
DV: Observed concentrations, PRED: population predictions of the model, IPRED: individual predictions of the model, Type_2: ADC, Type_3: MMAE.
The gray solid y=x or y=0 lines are included for reference. The bold red lines are the lowest (local regression smoother) trend lines.

Base (black vertical line and red text): predicted Cycle 6 exposure in a typical patient following 2.0 mg/kg Q3W doses (with 200 mg cap). Black shaded bar: exposure range correspond to 5th and 95th percentiles of exposure distribution. Blue shaded bars: influence of each single covariate on the Cycle 6 exposure. For each continuous covariate, two subjects were generated with extreme covariate values (2.5th and 97.5th percentile). For each categorical covariate, one subject from each category was created; other covariates were fixed at the reference category. The length of each bar describes the potential impact of that particular covariate on MMAE exposure, with the percentage values representing the percent change of exposure from the base. The most influential covariate is at the top of the plot.
